ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-25-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u1	Dear Members, fellow Members, distinguished Minister! Your Honors are here! I begin the continuation of the 34th meeting of the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia. I am informed that the following fellow Members and fellow Members are absent: Dr Michael Brejce, Ferry Horvat, Roman Jakič, Jelko Kacin, Jožef Kavtičnik, Ivan Kebric, Milan Kopuzar, Dorian Marcič and Alojz Peterle. I'm moving to seven. Period of the day - firstly, a proposal for a legal person's income tax. The draft law was submitted by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia for consideration by the assembly. In this regard, a group of ten Members with the first-signed Dr Andrey Bajuk requested that the State Assembly hold a general debate. I give the floor to the representative of the government, the Minister of Finance, Dr Dusan Maramor, for the introductory explanation of the bill. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-25-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u2	Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman. Dear Members and Members! Tax harmonisation within the European Union has so far been limited mainly to tax procedures and certain restrictions on facilitations, while in harmonising tax rates it applies only to certain excise duties. Thus, in the tax area, there is a tax competition between current and new members of the European Union and there are few prospects for this to change soon. The first key element of tax competitiveness is the total amount of taxes paid in the country and Slovenia is, according to the latest available data from 2001, below the average of the current EU member states with 39.7% of gross domestic product at 41.1% of the 15th. The five members have a lower relative scope of taxes, namely Portugal, Greece, Spain, Ireland and the United Kingdom, and ten higher. The lower relative scope is mainly the current members, who are more similar to Slovenia after their development. The reduction of this is, of course, important in terms of reducing budgetary expenditure. Another key element of tax competitiveness is the structure of taxes paid. Comparisons can be made in many different ways, but in terms of competitiveness for Slovenia there is a key comparison between the tax burden on labour and capital. If taken as an appropriate representation of the workload of the work of the Eurostat methodology, where it compares the share of taxes and contributions in labour costs to 67% of the average wage in the processing industry, then in 2002 Slovenia is around 40% strong over the average of the current 15th which has 37.8%. On average, Portugal, Greece and Spain have only 32.5% of the labour burden. The situation is reversed in the case of capital taxation, where, according to the latest comparable data for 2001, the share of the profit tax paid in the gross domestic product in Slovenia is 1.21% and 3.5% in the 15th. Only one country, Germany, had a smaller share of Slovenia in 2001, due to a strong economic recession. Slovenia also had one of the lowest actual, so-called effective tax rates on profit compared to EU members. It follows from the above, as well as from other data, that Slovenia is tax-free at work and very competitive in the taxation of corporate profits, but it has proved that such competition is not appropriate. Data on foreign investments in Slovenia and a study on the effects of so low profit taxation show that such a decision did not produce the desired results. On the other hand, the tax burden on work has proved to be very uncompetitive. They have already learned this from the government and Parliament and in 2003 increased the actual taxation of corporate profits to 12.7% by reducing tax reliefs. You have before you a proposal for a law on corporate income tax, which means the next phase by further reducing the benefits and increasing the actual tax rate at the estimated 17.8%, which is still lower or closer to the most competitive new members of our country, for example Slovakia with 19% without relief - Slovakia is the country that has gone to the most recently in tax competition. It is essential, however, that the increased amount of tax paid on the income of legal persons will be fully directed towards reducing the tax burden of work imposed by the proposal for a new law on income. However, the tax reliefs that remain in the proposal for a law on corporate income tax are substantially amended in the light of the current law. They are kept only for investments that typically mean an increase in value added, i.e. in production equipment, not in buildings, cars, office equipment and in particular for certain development equipment. Dear Members and Members, therefore, before you is the first of the tax laws aimed not only at complying with European directives but also at tax changes that will increase competitiveness [[...]] [[A warning sign for the end of the debate.]] the Slovenian economy of entry into the EU. [[...]] For these reasons, honourable Members and Members, the Government proposes that you take a decision in the proposal for a law suitable for further consideration. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-25-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u3	Thank you, Mr Minister, for this introductory explanation, for the additional explanation of the bill. ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-25-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u4	Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman. Dear colleagues and colleagues. The proposal for a Law on the Income of Legal Persons, which, at the request of the Group of Members and in accordance with the Rules of Procedure today, has been the subject of a first reading, has long ago been announced by the ruling coalition as part of what they have so far called a tax reform and whose published objective is to increase the taxation of capital revenues and reduce the taxation of labour income. Despite widespread propaganda, it is only the fact that, in the context of the so-called tax reform, the government has so far submitted to the National Assembly only this proposal, together with the Income Act. However, from this point of view alone, namely that the government has ensured that the two laws intend to achieve common objectives, both proposals should be addressed at the same time by this State Assembly. On the other hand, the government itself recognises in its own explanations how both laws are linked to the urgent changes in the Tax Procedure Act, which is not in the procedure at all. By separating their treatment in the national assembly, the government is deliberately avoiding clear treatment or presentation of the objectives of the so-called tax reform. Let me add that the projected effects of the amendments in both laws are already planned in the budgets adopted for 2004 and 2005. In a country that respects the principles of transparent fiscal and budgetary policy management, both laws should be adopted before their effects are planned in the budget, i.e. before the budget is adopted. However, any major change in the tax system is not an abstract design, which should only be based on the models that economic knowledge has developed over the decades, but rather on the specific reality of a particular company, and in our case, the undeniable fact that Slovenia is still deeply involved in the transition process and that the transformation of social property, regardless of how it was planned, has actually caused a large concentration of capital in the hands of a relatively small circle of people who have become extremely rich and therefore also have a certain political power. And when we talk about the concentration of capital in Slovenia, we cannot, by means of a fact confirmed in international labour and capital comparisons, that Slovenia has the lowest rate of capital taxation and one of the highest rates of labour taxation. The objectives of the changes in labour and capital taxation after the end of the transition period should be based on an accurate documented analysis of the last ten years of the taxation period. The government should present to the general Slovenian public and, of course, to this national council changes in the distribution of assets or capital at the analytical system level, but this information was not available to the Slovenian economic academic profession or to the public. Let me add that Professor of Public Finance, Dr. Tina Stanovnik, commented with the words: "This is a scandalous and sad fact." Secondly, the Government should present an analysis of the distribution and concentration of income of natural persons. Although the profession has published some such analyses, it says itself that such analyses have their shortcomings because they are based on surveys on the use of households. Thirdly, the government should present a comparative analysis of the taxation of capital and labour in the Member States of the European Union and future members in order to assess the necessary fiscal changes with a view to maintaining and promoting the competitiveness of the Slovenian economy. Instead, we note that there are no fundamental data bases in the draft laws for the so-called tax reform that are available to this national assembly to assess the current tax policy and assess the situation, nor to assess the objectives that we want to achieve through the so-called tax reform. The Law on the Income of Legal Persons and the Income Act should be adopted in order to comply with European guidelines and to implement the decisions of the Constitutional Court. The real tax reform is still ahead. An integral part of this tax reform is also the predicted change in additional health insurance to a social contribution that intends to impose even more on gross wages. It is therefore irresponsible, in our opinion, that the government has so far submitted proposals for changes to the tax regime as a tax reform. Tax reforms will not be carried out by a large coalition under the leadership of the Liberal Democracy of Slovenia, because in this case it would not be possible to preserve the protection of the transitional capitalists, who have become mainly in the grey or internal market and an unsupervised organised market with the fraud of small shareholders, allowing them to do so under the rule of Liberal Democracy, adopted legislation and non-functional supervisory institutions. What do we know about changes in ownership structure, equity and capital taxation in Slovenia during the transition period? Managers are already in the former social capital of companies with cca. 3.8% increased to at least 15% after the 2001 estimates. This 15% of the share of the capital held by managers does not include the ownership of the principally management layer in enterprises through the ownership of the PIDs or the funds created from them. Let me also mention that in these funds there is one percent of shareholders, higher issuers of certificates, which is 90% of the assets. We also have in Slovenia, as in Croatia or in Russia, great capitalists, who could be charged by Josef Stiglitz, a so-called transition tax, as proposed by Nobel Economist Josef Stiglitz. Of course, the Liberal Democracy and the United List will not do that. In his article published in our Finances on 15 December 2003, Nobel Prizewinner for Economics, Josef Stiglitz, warned at the time of the arrest of Russian typhoon Mikhail Hodorkovski that Russia should tax the capital gains of the oligarchs that had become part of the privatisation process of the former state capital. After Stiglitz, Russian oligarchs could also be taxed up to 90% of capital gains. On the contrary, he believes that Russia is threatening to turn capitalist oligarchs into political oligarchs and threaten the development of democracy. Taxes collected from capitalist oligarchs are intended to finance Russian health and education in Russia. Is there any similarity to Slovenia? An analysis of changes in the corporate capital ownership structure, which, when properly prepared for the so-called tax reform, the government should ensure that, according to our assessment, this would be confirmed in many ways. In its 2003 development report, the Office for Macroeconomic Analysis and Development published an analysis of the modified ownership composition of owned enterprises during the period 1994-2001. This is the only publicly published analysis that allows at least partial insight into the transitional concentration of capital. It showed that during that period the total equity share of the institutions of the State, the parastatal funds, the CAD and the SOD and the PID or investment funds decreased from 48.6% to 25.5% in total capital. At the same time, ownership of domestic strategic owners, shares of companies in other companies, of 25.5%, increased almost equally. These were purchased mainly from KAD and SOD, such as the Pivovarna Union, Pivovarna Laškoa. The ownership of domestic owners, that is to say employees and in particular former employees, pensioners, decreased from 40.2% to 24.8. The shares were sold primarily to the management leader, so their share of 3.8 went up to 15.5, as I mentioned. The study of Umar has shown, and it is also generally known that management structures have increased their share mainly in internal and grey markets where share prices have been strongly underestimated. This is common knowledge. However, shares and shares of unfair value have also been acquired in other ways that enabled them to implement and implement the transitional arrangements. The privatisation process therefore results in a significant increase in the ownership of the leading management of companies and domestic strategic owners, as the shares of both rose from 5.8% to 41%, making them the most important representatives of the capital elite. If the finding Dies that the management structure has a direct 15% share of the equity of owned enterprises, and this is data in 2001, and this has increased, translated or converted into a book value of capital of equity of enterprises at the end of 2002 to 2,551 billion, we get an estimate that managers today have about 400 billion book value of capital in owned enterprises. Since only a dozen of these companies are listed in the market market, it is not possible to assess the fair market value of this 400 billion book value of the management capital. PIDI, these are, of course, assessments, but the government, one that would truly support and rule the interests of all citizens, could document these assessments with accurate data, as they are available to institutions as a clearing company, AIPES and others. This analysis should therefore be ordered today by independent experts selected in the invitation to tender by this national assembly - this would be a decision, but of course it will not be adopted. As we know, in other countries, capital is taxed mainly in two ways, by taxation of capital gains and by taxation of dividends or taxes on the profits of their owners. And how do we tax capital gains in Slovenia? The majority of citizens have acquired huge assets in transition, their capital gains are taxed only on the sale of shares or the payment of shares and the replacement of capital within a period of less than three years, at a tax rate of 30%. For the sale of shares and shares of natural persons after three years, the capital gains are no longer taxed. So far, taxation of the capital gains of natural persons after three years at zero rates has been introduced with the aim of ensuring that citizens remain shareholders of the companies as long as possible and that the share of portfolio capital owners is not too high. As we see, these objectives have not been met, so there are no professional reasons at least to maintain such a liberal regime in the future. However, Slovenia is included in the countries which derive at least tax revenue from this source by taxing the capital gains of natural persons. In 2001, the total value of all income sources amounted to 2,469 billion tolars, of which only 64 million tolars of capital profit from the sale of shares or capital shares. And how much does Slovenia collect in total property taxes compared to other countries? In 2001, there were only 1.6 billion tolls of property taxes in income sources of 2469 billion, of which 64 capital gains were mentioned and the remaining amount was mainly a compensation for the use of building land. We must not forget that during the transition period we also had a massive privatisation of the social housing property, but that the country is collecting negligible income from capital gains. In 2001, 15 countries in the European Union averaged 2% of their gross domestic product taxes, the average of the OECD countries was 1.9%, while our country only collected 0.6% of their gross domestic product taxes, a third. Between developed OECD countries and transition countries, most of them have less favourable taxation of capital gains than Slovenia. Countries such as Sweden, Finland, Norway, France, Japan, Ireland, Hungary, Italy, Iceland have a uniform rate of taxation of capital gains, irrespective of the time of sale of shares or shares. After a certain period, capital gains are taxed at a lower rate in the United States, Great Britain and Spain. However, we also have countries that have a zero level after a certain period. For example, they are neighbouring Austria and Germany, but it must be taken into account and stressed that in these multi-century capitalist regimes, capital gains do not arise by damaging small shareholders in unsecured markets or by so-called initial capital accumulation, as was obviously the case in the last decade with us in Slovenia. A large coalition led by the Liberal Democracy has decided to maintain the current system of taxation of capital gains, with the sale of shares and shares after three years, the tax rate will continue to be zero and up to 30% by three years. This means, for example, that when Mr Jankovic sold his shares in Mercator at the rate of SIT - at least this is what some media report - if he were in Ireland or Finland, Sweden, Italy, etc., he would pay between 20% and 30% of the capital gains tax, but in Slovenia he will not, because he will, of course, wait three years for the sale. For example, the three-year period, as is well known, has already been used by Banka Koper’s shareholders, when they sold the shares to the Italian owner exactly three years after their cheap purchases. Their capital gains were then ten times higher than the purchase value of the shares. However, the price of shares was directly raised by Drnovskov's government when the public debt took over the loan of the Bank's largest commissioner, the General Sailing of Piran, amounting to 75 million U.S. dollars. This capital gain was donated to these shareholders by the government under the leadership of the Liberal Democracy, and in fact it has been placed on the shoulders of all taxpayers, current and future. So if we come from an assessment that managers in companies today have approximately cca. The 400 billion capital of bookkeeping value, and this capital, or its resulting capital gains, would still be taxed with a transition tax, as proposed today by the world's most established economist, Joseph Stiglitz, for example, for only 10% and not with the 90's as he proposes for Russia, could easily cover the entire cumulative public deficit of our health treasury, approx. 40 billion at the end of 2004. And if we also taxed the capital gains of the minority in the PIDs or the funds from them, with approximately the same amount of capital, we could cover a significant proportion of the deficits in other state-owned stores, such as the SPIZ. And what is the profit tax on doing business or dividends? We also find in the taxation of profit tax compared to other countries that in fact we also have the least burdened capitalists in this respect. We are severely lagging behind in the taxation of the profits of legal persons. In 2000, the share of corporate income tax in the 15 countries of the European Union averaged 3.8% of gross domestic product. The average share in OECD countries was 3.6 in us was 1.2. The nominal rate of profit tax is 25% for us, effective or effective taxationp only 12.4. While in less developed transition countries, the Czech Republic, Slovakia, Poland and Hungary averaged 15.7, in the 15th European Union on average 26.8%, i.e. more than twice as high as Slovenia. If we are not yet to compare ourselves with the developed countries, of course we have to deal with those who join us in the EU. In the transition decade in Slovenia, this profit is also from business, and not just capital gains, among the least taxed in international comparisons. And what is wage taxation compared to other countries? The burden of income from work is in Slovenia compared to the developed countries among the highest. The ten-year rule of the Liberal Democracy has brought Slovenia into a position to have wages among the most heavily burdened and most burdened. According to the OECD, Slovenia was classified in 2002 as one of the countries with the most taxed middle-class gross wages. On average, a productive worker in processing activities had to deduct 33.2% of the income earned to the State and the social security contribution remaining 66.8 from the gross earnings earned to the State and its net salary. In the EU, Denmark, Belgium and Germany are ahead of us, and behind us are all the other economically developed and also the transition countries. The average share of income and social transfers in gross wages reached 27.6% in the 15th EU and is therefore significantly lower than the wage burden of the Slovenian middle class. Even less taxed were salaries in four transition countries, the Czech Republic, Slovakia, Poland and Hungary, where the average share of income and social contributions was 25.7%, not 33.6 in our country. The proposed Income Tax Act provides for a higher effective taxation of profits, a nominal rate of 25%, thus reducing incentives for investment investments in real estate, buildings, land. The government notes in its analysis that so far the real estate relief has not yielded the expected results and has not accelerated the development of activities with a higher added value. This finding by the Government, in principle or at an average level, is certain to be trusted, since so far the facilitation of investment investments has not been properly regulated, has been recognised to all companies, irrespective of the start of their activities, has not been linked to the creation of new jobs or to the growth of the scope of activities. The new companies, as even the government itself has reported, could not have benefited from this relief because they had not yet generated a profit for taxation and therefore we had a so-called neutral tax relief to invest in real estate. In fact, such tax relief arrangements have greatly benefited transitional capitalists, relatively large and also unjustifiable real estate tax reliefs, according to tax experts, have often been reduced by an effective tax rate also due to the abuse of such facilities, such as housing facilities, in practice, as a result of the lack of and non-professional supervision of the tax administration of the Republic of Slovenia. In our view, tax reliefs in investments that remain under the new law or equipment should be linked to the actual establishment of new companies, to the opening of new jobs and to the expansion of income and profit activities. In setting up or developing new companies in activities with a higher added value, reliefs in real estate and not only in equipment may also be useful. In addition to this comprehensive and unconvincing investment relief, we keep it at 10% and the proposal contains a novelty in terms of promoting R & D investment, since the commitment under Article 49(2) may impose an additional reduction in the tax base of 10% of the investment in R & D equipment. It is precisely because this proposal is a novelty in our tax legislation that we expected that the government, in its explanations of the proposal, would not only give professional reasons for its decision to introduce such additional relief, i.e. to justify the specific features of this proposal which it has decided to propose to this national assembly. This is a matter of professional justification, which is of great importance in the light of the long experience of other countries, and why we seek them in vain in the explanations given by the government in its proposal to this National Assembly. Moreover, the explanation given is shorter than the second paragraph of Article 49 itself, which introduces this relief. In a rather extensive analysis published by the OECD last year, we find that as many as 18 Member States of these organisations have fiscal incentives for research and development. However, many of them are introducing certain reforms into their already existing systems and mechanisms with the aim of increasing their effectiveness, which has not always been in line with expectations. On the basis of this analysis, there is a legitimate question why our government has decided to propose as a basis for this additional facilitation a certain part of the investment in equipment needed for research and development, and not the current expenditure as used in all 18 OECD countries with fiscal incentives for this purpose. Of course, some additionally include investments in expenditure, that is to say, expenditure on equipment. Let me add an interesting picture of this analysis, namely that so-called current expenditure represents around 90% of all R & D expenditure. Why did the government decide to invest only 10% in equipment? On the other hand, the question arises why the government in this case proposes a reduction in the tax base, a deduction before the tax base or "taxa lawens" and not a reduction in the tax liability "tax credit". Countries in the OECD that have used in practice a mechanism to reduce the tax base for these reliefs shall decide to reduce the tax liability. There is also a question of why the government has decided to reduce the tax base of 10% of investment in R & D equipment over a certain period and not part of the additional expenditure above the average of the last years or an incremental approach, such as in the United States, France, Japan, Mexico and Korea. Of course, there is a third option: a combined partial write-off system of investments or just a write-off of additional investments over a certain average, such as Australia, Austria, Spain and Portugal. The government did not explain why it did not include any differentiation in its proposal for certain companies, such as small and medium-sized enterprises, such as practice in many OECD countries. Or certain activities, such as partnerships between companies and public research institutions and universities. Experiences in OECD countries are rich in these areas and in Nova Slovenija and Nova Slovenija we cannot accept the government proposal until professional justification is presented to us and the general public. The explanations of the government proposal also completely disregard the analysis of the expected effectiveness of the proposed tax incentives compared to direct subsidies, which is also a widespread practice in OECD countries. However, the empirical research mentioned in the OECD comparative analysis shows that tax incentives for research and development may on average increase the company’s R & D expenditure for the sum of approximately the same as for the resulting tax fall. An analysis of 19-year data in 9 OECD countries showed that 10% of the tax subsidy for R & D was only a 1% increase in these expenditure in the short term, while in the long term it almost achieved this surplus but did not exceed 10%. In Canada alone, empirical analyses have shown that one dollar of R & D relief has caused $1.38 for such expenditure in companies that have used these facilitations. However, we must note that the Canadian system is in many ways different from the government's proposed scheme of tax reduction and reliefs represent 13.3% of the total expenditure of R & D companies not being 10% invested in equipment and operating in combination with direct subsidies representing 22.7% of the total expenditure of R & D companies. In 2001, the Belgian Minister of Finance's Financial Policy High Council proposed the lifting of all concessions in this area, including in order to ensure the neutrality of the tax system. If the government does not give professional reasons for its proposal, the Coalition Slovenia will propose the deletion of Article 49, paragraph 2. The proposed Income Tax Act extends the tax base from the existing taxable profit to other types of income, with other sources of income - Article 8 proposed, which is of course necessary and also requires the harmonisation of the law with the guidelines of the European Union. However, it is important to stress that the concepts defining these sources are not sufficiently clearly defined and that if such provisions were adopted, they would lead to difficulties in implementing the new law. Any source of income should be - this is the term - the law should specify precisely. The news brought by the new law in the calculation of the income tax is yet another thing. In this general debate, it is only necessary to point out that the law leaves too many matters to the regulation of sub-legislative acts. Despite the fact that, due to the current regulation and its repeal by the Constitutional Court and the various treatments of the tax administration of the Republic of Slovenia of the Administrative Court, there are a number of problems in taxing profits in practice, such as the identification of business and non-business costs prior to the determination of a taxable profit. That is why, Mr Finance Minister, we expect you to contribute to the public presentation of opinions on this law as well as the law on income in the National Assembly being joined by representatives as widely as possible by the professional public. At the end of the citizens and citizens, let us also make it clear that the large coalition led by the Liberal Democracy with the Income Act and the Income Act does not in fact propose a tax reform to tax capital more heavily and to relieve the middle class of employees who carried and still bear the heavy burden of this transition. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-25-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u6	Mr. Vice President, thank you very much for your words. Dear Secretary of State, advisors to the government, distinguished colleagues and colleagues, welcome. In the UK Group of Social Democrats, we foresee that the drafters of the law have taken into account the necessary changes to the law, which are identified by the problems identified in cases of tax controls that are so general or unconscionable that they allow the tax authority to interpret and thus apply them differently in practice and that we are often reminded of by the economic operators concerned and of the Constitutional Court of the Republic of Slovenia regarding the legal security of taxable persons. The amendment of the Law on corporate tax after 2002 regulated the provision of legal certainty for taxable persons by respecting constitutional principles and making it clear from the law what the State requires of a taxable person. Other lessons from the period of implementation of the corporate tax system, which are also linked to respect, the release of investments and capital concentrations, the high mobility of capital and the development of new technologies, also require adjustments to the corporate income tax system in order to protect the tax base. It is appropriate to renovate some of the institutions already introduced, in particular the definition of the debtor, business units, residents and the like. The consideration of that law should also take into account a very important reason for issuing a new law, which is the commitment of the Republic of Slovenia to align the legislation of the Republic of Slovenia with the legal system of the European Union. In the UK Group of Social Democrats, we believe that only addressing the comprehensive tax package could allow prudent and professional and politically acceptable solutions, as this is a difficult and sensitive reform, especially since it is to be adopted in the last year, this is in the electoral year. However, the need for reform cannot be linked to the mandate of the legislative body. In the UK Group of Social Democrats, we are convinced that it is possible to initiate the adoption of tax legislation also on the basis of substantial fundamental grounds for issuing a new law, as stated by the bill-maker. However, we are aware that it is impossible to expect Members and Members not to compete in the electoral year with proposals for various facilitations and with a review of the systemic aspect of equality of the debtors, as different warnings are already running in this direction. Given that the law at first reading, which means that there will be plenty of time for individual suggestions, however, we have also drawn up certain views or comments in the UK Group on the proposal for a law on corporate tax, which the applicant should examine and, of course, take due account of our conviction. In this way, I would like to express some of our views, in particular Article 10, which also refers to individual legal provisions regarding the subject matter under taxation. However, it should be mentioned here that the drafter of the law provided in this article that the tax year could be the same for a business year. In doing so, the condition should be laid down that the taxable person must apply the tax treatment of legal persons' incomes for seven years. It is our position and therefore I am also making this proposal that time should be limited to five tax years. Second. Our concerns also refer to Article 13; these are tax bases for business between connected persons, residents. We propose that the first paragraph of Article 13 be supplemented in the sense that the opposite option is also taken into account. That is why, of course, we also propose that this new text of Article 1 should also be read - I will quote: "In determining the income and expenditure of employees and debtors, residents connected to persons, the tax base does not increase, is added or reduced. Where a reduction or increase in the tax base in one taxpayer is added, the same increase or reduction of the tax base in the other taxpayer is added in relation to a particular business between them and such determination of income and expenditure does not result in a reduction in the overall tax effect." In fact, we estimate that this supplement is needed mainly because of a clearer and even more unambiguous explanation, since in practice today there have been many cases where the tax authority on the basis of the same transaction increases the income of one taxpayer while at the same time failing to increase the expenditure of another taxpayer, which results in double taxation. We were also reminded of this by individual tax advisers. We also have some concerns about Article 15 of the Act, which speaks of interest between connected persons. In determining the recognised interest rate, the applicant provided that it was also necessary to take into account the loans received by the resident of the Republic of Slovenia and abroad and that a recognised interest rate should be fixed for each foreign country or group of foreign countries. While we can be sure that this can also be regulated by a sub-legislative act, it is a sub-legislative act to be laid down by the Minister, but until it is undoubtedly written and recorded in the law, we believe that this part of Article 15 should be clarified, so that we have a coherent and understandable Article 15. We also have a point of view in Article 26, which is the part where in paragraphs 7 and 8 it is also necessary to decide how to proceed if a resident acquires an old basic asset from a connected and unrelated person who is a resident. In paragraphs 7 and 8, only cases are currently settled where the underlying asset is obtained from a connected or unrelated person who is not a resident. Well, we believe that paragraphs 7 and 8 should also be approximated textually, as they are now written very unusually and also difficult to understand. Indeed, we also take into account the fact, or the position of the Ministry of Finance, that this proposal could not somehow be taken into account, because it is precisely by the proposal of the law that it means what is written down and that the general provision applies to residents, but as far as we take a closer look at these general provisions, we can see that there is no place to see anything similar to what is settled for non-residents, which is why we believe it is necessary to write this into the law itself. We also have a suggestion, even in the light of Article 49, that is in that part of the law which speaks of tax incentives for investment and also for innovative activity. Well, the fact is that this text of the law also takes account of economic preferences in these tax incentives for investment, an additional 10% reduction in the tax base for investment in R & D equipment, but it is precisely in this relief that we think it is necessary to listen to the economy too, because it is to be achieved that this relief is too narrow. Research and development are only part of the whole innovation process, and therefore the existing same tax incentives should also make it possible to invest in equipment that is also dedicated to innovation. Therefore, we also propose that, in preparing for second reading, Article 49, paragraph 3, which states that the taxable person may apply an additional reduced tax base in the amount of [[...]] [[Incomprehensible.]] the amount invested in R & D equipment has also been added to the words 'and innovation'. It is also necessary to comply with public administration laws that were later adopted, for example, the State Administration Act, the Civil Servants Act, the Inspection Control Act, the Public Finance Act, the General Administrative Procedure, the Public Funds Act, the Agencies. Furthermore, it is a legislative proposal to harmonise the law with the recommendations and guidelines of the Council of Europe, to enforce the principles of the European Charter on Local Self-Government in the national legal order, and it is not too much to say that the proposed law is one of the European laws, as it regulates the new electoral rights of foreigners for members of the local councils, actively and passively, and for mayors only the active electoral rights of foreigners. The amendments concern the following areas. First, to consolidate the situation of local self-government in the public administration system, secondly, the right to information, consultation and participation of residents in public affairs, because it is a matter of strengthening participation at local level. Next, the withdrawal of legal entity status to the larger parts of the municipality, local, village and fourth communities. Then complement the relationship between the representative bodies, i.e. the municipal council and the executive body, the mayors. The following concerns the incompatibility between the public functions of the legislative, executive and administrative branches of government with functions in local self-government, in particular the incompatibility of the Member and the mayor. Furthermore, the law newly regulates the issue of common municipal administrations, when they are mandatory, in order to meet the legal requirements. In particular, the new law is governed by the responsibility of the self-government local community for damage and the limited regressive liability of compensation to the official. The measures of the State authorities relating to the supervision of the legality of the work of the local authorities are also regulated by law and the procedure for the change of the territory of the municipality and financial supervision. Allow me to mention briefly just a few of the most important innovations, those that have emerged as the most up-to-date in the debates so far. The first relates to the local, lice and fourth communities, which are located in the upper part of the congregation. The proposal for regulation of the formation of smaller fourth and village communities and towns is based on the aim of improving the efficiency of management in the municipality. Greater involvement of citizens in the management of local affairs, or closer implementation of tasks for citizens. Therefore, the law does not, of course, abolish local communities and other communities, but only proposes or proposes that the community no longer has legal personality status. Legal subjectivity is not a condition for strengthening the local community as a form of direct democracy at local level, in our belief. The second novelty relates to the relationship between the representative body, the municipal council and the mayor. The bill contains a provision stipulating that the mayor participates in the decision-making of the municipal council also by voting in favour of the decisions. This situation gives him the same rights and, above all, the same responsibilities for the decisions taken by the congregation council as other members of the congregation council. I have already mentioned that one of the novelties of the law is the mutual incompatibility of public functions. This is based on constitutional principles, namely the principle of the division of power, which highlights the independence of the legislative, executive and judicial branches of government and the principle of the independence and autonomy of local self-government. As is known, the law introduces the incompatibility of the MP and the mayor with the following elections. It would also include the joint provision of the tasks of the municipal administration and public powers in this regard, the follow-up by the State authorities to the supervision of the legality of the work, in accordance with the 2003 Constitutional Court decision, as well as the amended procedure for the changes in the territory of the municipality. Ladies and gentlemen, I propose that, following the debate, the law, as proposed, be supported and thus made possible its adoption after further consideration. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-25-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u37	Thank you very much for your words, Madam Vice President. Dear Minister, representatives of the government, colleagues and colleagues! The fundamental constitutional guarantee and the definition of local self-government in the Constitution is one of the most important constitutional values. Local self-government is a legal institution that defines the position of the local community, is an integral and proportionate independent part of public administration in the country and is a institution of decentralised authority, which means the establishment of a democratic principle of the division of power in a vertical sense. Its self-government status is therefore strengthened by the elements of the brakes and the balance between it and the State authorities. Recommendations to the governments of the Member States of the European Union by the Council of Europe ministerial committee should aim at developing a policy to promote citizens' participation in local public life, to improve the legal basis for cooperation and to enable local authorities to make a wide range of means of cooperation and to commit themselves to more effective promotion of citizens' participation in local public life. The nature of relations in the local community is based on the close integration of people in the community and greater participation of citizens in the exercise of local authority. Therefore, the right to direct decision on the most important issues is an integral part of local self-government. In the local community, it is much easier to use different direct decision-making institutions than in the country because of the smaller area and the number of inhabitants and their connection to the immediate vital interests and needs. Moreover, the ability of citizens to make direct decisions also increases their interest in participating in local self-government matters, strengthening its effectiveness and contributing to greater legitimacy of the decisions taken. The possibility of direct decision-making allows the full exercise of the human right to participate in the enforcement of public affairs. Thus, at the declaration level, in the sense of such and other highly plausible words, which take up too much space in the bill. The basic rule governing the system of local self-government in our country is the 1993 law on local self-government, which has undergone many changes and additions over the years and several judgments by the Constitutional Court. As such, it was the beginning of a reform of local self-government, which, according to the new law's proponents, was completed. In view of these reasons, in our view, it is not necessary to adopt a new law, but to bring it into line with the Constitution and the European Charter on Local Self-Government. The basic difference between the existing law and the proposed law is in the concept of the law itself. The existing law deals with the area of local self-government in the principle-open spirit of the Constitution and the European Charter of Local Self-Government on autonomy and the principle of subsidiarity and guarantees the rights to local self-government, while this proposal abolishes it. In particular, this is reflected in the deletion of all articles which discuss the conditions and possibilities for the establishment of new municipalities. At the same time, it abolishes the only substitute, which is the status of a legal person in the lower parts of the congregation. The proposal even forces municipalities under 3,000 people to give up their administration and exercise their independence and to establish joint municipal administrations. The difficulty of the proposal before us is not really what is mentioned in the introduction, but the actual and definitive halting of the process of maturation of local self-government in Slovenia and stabilisation based on the work and will of citizens and citizens. The proposal as a basic act of local self-government does not contain a starting point for the provision of funds for the implementation of tasks entrusted to the municipalities which are inconsistent and left to another law in the law. It also facilitates the participation of municipalities or their representatives in the process of adopting acts which concern them, as is the case in the law in force. Such an approach is not in line with the European Charter on Local Self-Government and the recommendations of the Congress of Local and Regional Authorities of the Council of Europe. The proposed law interferes with the relationship between the authorities themselves in the local community, for which there is no basis in any of the original arguments. The bill provides for more stringent conditions, according to the existing proposal of the law, that for reasons of geographical, border, ethnic, historical and economic reasons the municipality may also have an exception to less than 5,000 inhabitants, which the law no longer allows. However, the proposal for a law still contains a number of open issues, which, of course, we have highlighted. All of this must be borne in mind that local self-government needs to be adapted to European guidelines. One of the main features is that the local self-government is the institution of decentralised authority to promote and accelerate the participation of citizens in local public life and, above all, to ensure the participation and participation of citizens in the creation and operation of the local level, so that residents themselves as decision-makers can manage their own affairs or directly and indirectly participate in the management of public affairs. ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-25-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u45	Dear Minister! A respected choir! Allow me to present to you the position of the Slovenian Democratic Party Group on the proposal for a law on local self-government. The proposal for a new law in local self-government sent to the legislative process by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia represents the greatest and gravest attempt so far to interfere with local self-government, which was introduced on the basis of the new Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia and the Law on Local Self-Government in 1994. However, the proposal for a law was forwarded to the procedure at a time which is objectively already pre-election period, and the intervention is therefore a sensitive area such as local self-government, which needs to be assessed in particular from this point of view. Nevertheless, the basic feature of this government proposal is to pursue only two objectives. First, the legislative proposal follows an increase in the potential for the direct and indirect impact of the State and authorities on the developments in individual municipalities and thus the remarrying of the area of actual local self-government. The second objective of the legislative proposal is to reduce the possibility of citizens directly participating in the creation of local self-government and to reduce the possibility of citizens participating in the procedures. The two objectives, in our view and our belief, have been completely wrongly set, and therefore the starting point of the law for our parliamentary group, as well as the whole issue, has already been disputed. In particular, some concrete solutions are missing, some of which are uncontestedly constitutionally disputed. The proposed law does not contain any more criteria or criteria for the establishment of new municipalities, and in individual cases, the decision remains with the Government of the Republic of Slovenia. Moreover, in the transitional and final provisions, it repeals a special law that has more precisely regulated procedures for the establishment of new municipalities. The purpose of the arbitral decision of the executive branch on the right to local self-government or administrative persuasion of the establishment of new smaller municipalities is therefore clear, although this right is provided for in the Constitution and is also protected by international acts. Let me mention only the European Charter on Local Self-Government. In comparison, I would like to point out that until the forced establishment of the Cardidian communes in the territory of Slovenia, all from the Austro-Hungarian number of municipalities in this area has been at least twice as large as it is now. The proposed law allows for an extremely wide range of enforcement interventions in the field of local self-government. This is a whole range of measures allowing central, state authorities, inter alia, to enforce individual measures at the expense of the municipality. They shall prevent the adoption of acts at the level of the municipality by which the state authority would disagree and shall even allow the exercise of the direct personal, material and other responsibilities of the holders of functions in local self-government. This is very controversial for the Slovenian Democratic Party Group. The government proposal states that the government can also call off municipal officials, the most important one, which is to the mayor, but can also limit income or compensation for the performance of the function and the performance of the function in full. It is probably too much to point out that this is suggested by the government, which does not even come to mind with regard to the responsibility of ministers, even in cases where there is a clear violation of the laws. I would like to remind you that, for example, Minister Gaber peacefully admitted years ago that he was aware of a violation of labour law at the Ministry under his leadership. Nothing happened. No sanctions, no consequences. Given that we live in a country where there is yet another policy, one policy option, all-powerful and all-encompassing, it is clear that the intention to legislate these proposals would almost certainly lead to attempts at political abuse. However, once such solutions have been remedied, one of the fundamental requirements of the European Charter of Local Self-Government would no longer be met, whereby the essential function of local self-government is to foster effective protection of local communities against arbitrary or arbitrary interference by the central government. The government proposal for a law also provides a totally unacceptable double increase in the number of mandatory signatures to hold a referendum at the level of the local community, from 5 to 10% of all voters in the municipality. And in this case, it is an obvious continuation of the policy of abolishing the actual possibility of a referendum, as it is clear that it is essentially an unattainable number. In comparison, I should point out that according to this logic, some 200,000 signatures should be collected for the request for a national referendum, which is a complete absurdity. The law also adds a political bone in the form of incompatible mayoral and parliamentary functions. It is clear that this proposal is intended to draw opinions in the political arena in particular at this point, instead of focusing more attention on the inadmissibility of interventions to local self-government. There is also no legal basis for the proposal to legislate non-conformity, since, under the other legal regime of the State, incompatibility should be dealt with and laid down by law which otherwise governs the non-conformity of a Member State's function with others. It is likely that for some kind of comforting award to this added provision, after which the mayors could also participate in the decision-making of the municipal council, which is, in particular, a completely different way of choosing and exercising the mayor's powers, a system of controversial and questionable solutions. The government's proposal for a new law on local self-government therefore constitutes an unacceptable intervention in the autonomy of local self-government. The foundations, and above all this is crucial, on the assumption that the reform of local self-government at first stage has already been completed, which is an undeniablely wrong starting point. Such a starting point is obviously also inappropriate in terms of the right to local self-government, as provided for by the Slovenian Constitution, as well as in terms of the legal principle of equality before the Constitution and the law. The proposed transfer of the key powers of the National Assembly from the field of local self-government to government is unacceptable and is also constitutionally questionable, since it essentially constitutes an executive power. As a whole, the government's proposal for a new law on local self-government is essentially an even greater centralisation of government, while it is also extremely undemocratic about some solutions. The proposed law on local self-government is undoubtedly also an obvious attack on the initial reform of local self-government in the Republic of Slovenia. This reform of local self-government is undoubtedly one of those steps and actions of Slovenian policy and our everyday one that have enabled and helped Slovenia to develop faster and more evenly over the past decade. There is no doubt that, because of the development and functioning of local communities, a fair share of what we have created has been distributed more fairly because of the good functioning of the municipalities, and because of this the countryside has also received the work of these goods. Such development, such divisions without newly established congregations, would certainly not be possible. In our view, the proposal for a law on local self-government is, once again, an unacceptable intervention in the autonomy of local self-government, based on the wrong assumption that the reform of local self-government has already been completed at the first stage. It is based on indisputably wrong assumptions and is essentially an attempt to further centralise local administrations as part of the state administration. The proposed transfer of the key powers of the National Assembly from the field of local self-government to the Government of the Republic of Slovenia is unacceptable. Slovenian Democrats believe that the legislative proposal is in sharp contradiction with our programming guidelines and that it must be rejected as such. We in the party of Slovenian Democrats advocate for a different policy, for as much autonomy as possible and for universal autonomy in the field of local self-government, which is crucial for the balanced and balanced development of Slovenia. Therefore, the Slovenian Democratic Party's parliamentary group will not support the proposal for a new law on local self-government. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-25-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u104	Dear Mr. President! The government has drafted a new law on the employment rehabilitation and employment of persons with disabilities. The new law was being prepared to mention, almost over 10 years ago, since the law in force extended to 1976, so the law was written for a different social arrangement, a different economic situation written during the full-time employment period, if I may say so. However, terminology has also been changed and, above all, new approaches in the field of employment, rehabilitation and understanding of disability. The necessity of adopting a new law also requires a new regulation of working relationships, a new system in the field of disability insurance under the law on pension and disability insurance and safety and health at work. In the last 10 years, the employment sector of persons with disabilities has been governed mainly by an active employment policy programme and by the establishment of disabled companies. However, there were no other solutions, so all too many people with disabilities have also seen problems in their application to the employment and social security agency, including through the pension disability insurance institution. These findings, even more than those of unemployed persons in the institution, are confirmed mainly by the structure of unemployed persons, which shows that 76% of the disabled are long-term unemployed, among them 86% of the disabled, 60% of the unemployed, according to age structure and relatively high insurance benefits, and 55% with only the first level of education. These are facts that show that the problem cannot be solved only through the APZ programmes or active employment policies, but that the disabled need something more. Therefore, the law introduces a comprehensive system whereby, for the first time, we increase the employability of persons with disabilities by means of employment rehabilitation, secondly, we increase the possibility of suitable employment for persons with disabilities in suitable jobs, with the possibility of adapting working time, with the possibility of providing other appropriate work, also with another employer, with employment in protective employment, with support and with disabled companies, we promote employment for shorter working hours, at home, work at a distance, work with information, communication and other support technology of equipped jobs, and, thirdly, we provide greater protection in employment for persons with disabilities. We also introduce ways to encourage, not just forcing employers to increase the employment of disabled people by introducing a quota system and various financial incentives for employers and disabled workers. We have followed good practice in the European Union, as well as in us, in the drafting of the law. We have taken into account the international recommendations and Directive 2000/78 on the introduction of a general policy for equal treatment and employment, and have taken into account the fact that integration and social partnership are the only way to address the issues of employment of persons with disabilities in an integrated way. Therefore, we have coordinated the law very wide and for almost 8 months before the government hearing with the Economic and Social Council, so that the social partners initialled on 19.11.2003 a declaration of consistency between the texts of the articles of the law. Secondly, to the Council of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia for Persons with Disabilities, which addressed the proposal for a law at a larger number of meetings, and for the third time with professional institutions and disability organisations. The purpose of the law is not only to hire a disabled person and thereby to reduce unemployment, but to employ a disabled person in appropriate jobs. Let me conclude: the law provides the basis for creating equivalent employment opportunities for people with disabilities and introduces an integration model in the employment of persons with disabilities instead of the now dominant so-called compensation model. Thank you very much.

This is also said to require flexibility on this side, and the law should leave open the door to some innovative capture of this problem. The quota system set up here is, of course, very good in the example of foreign countries. However, I must say that it is not understandable how the quota size will adapt to the employer. I mean, it's 1-6%. That is to say, if the employer has at least 20 employees, he will pay, if he does not have any disabled person, he will pay at least one per cent or 6 per cent of the total number of employees, at 70% of the minimum wage for each disabled person who should have an employee. So if the number two percent were valid, then 20 employees or so: if there were a five percent quota, 20 employees should have one disabled person. If I didn't have an employee, I'd be paying 70% of the minimum wage I'd give to a disabled person. The system is well placed, but there are two errors or two shortcomings. One, let me say again on a case from many years ago, like I did in Litostrom. There was an analysis of the specific job, which was said to be cleaners. These are the ones that cleaned the castings of the lithostrotic turbine and similar things after the X-rays, when they discovered, say, the cracks in the cast iron with the flexaries, they drilled, like, dental seals and so on. And that in some lobby, the hall used to make 20 people. That was terrible: noise, rust, sparks were flying all over the place, smells, smoke and so on. When I was doing an analysis 20 years ago of workers in these jobs, it was found that no one had endured this job for more than 12 years. After 12 years, he became disabled in the first category. At the latest, after 12 years of work, due to the vibration disorder, the failure of the nervous system of the neck ring, the nervous system failed, the wrists failed, the vibrational disease. And there's only one thing that would stop it -- the fluctuation. You know what this means after 12 years of disability in the first category: for the company nothing, get rid of it, get a young worker, and for the country it's a terrible burden. These were 30-, 35-year-old men, who, of course, went into disability in the first category, disabled. And if it were built into the whole mechanism of some motivation that the employer would have to do something to prevent this disability, then surely the company would have made a plan, a program and it would be very easy to make a fluctuation: after one month it goes to another job and so on and it would not have come to that. Because after six months, that transient damage of the organism is being rehabilitated again. In the law on safety at the work of this protection, there is no such motivation. There are some penalties, but that's not built up. And in this case, look at the quota system, first of all it is necessary to ask whether such a employer producing a series of invalids will be more burdened by a quota. While this is not in law, it is likely that the legal act or institution envisaged will take this into account. But if this is done, surely the employer will pay this quota in any case, because it will be for the activity and will pay the quota no matter how much in the following year or in the current year he will produce the disabled. And there'll be some kind of silence again. I don't like that. And it would be good to study this thing further. And there's another thing. If in some way we installed prevention of disability more than subsequent resolution, we would, of course, place all the burden on capital and less on wages. Because the quota that the employer will pay will go according to the number of employees, it will be attributed to the workload. It will be attributed to what we say that we are already the most burdened in Europe. Our wages are the most burdened in Europe. And that's the flaw we don't like. So, as I said, of course, we support the law as a suitable basis for further discussion, and we hope that there will be something else to add in this law and also to implement some amendments. Thank you very much.
